STATISTICAL ACCOUNT

OF THE

DISTRICT OF GOALPARA

(INCLUDING THE EASTERN DWARS). 
itil eee

OALPARA is the most westerly District of the Chief-Com- 
missionership of Assam, occupying the entrance to the 
Assam Valley. It lies along both banks of the Brahmaputra river, 
extending from 25° to 27° north latitude, and from go° to 91° east 
longitude. It contains an area, including the Eastern Dwdrs, which 
were annexed to the District in 1866, of 4433 square miles, and a 
population of 444,761 souls. The Civil Station, which is also the 
chief town of the District, is Godlpard, situated on the left or south 
bank of the Brahmaputra river, in 26° 10’ 25” north latitude, and 
go° 40’ 50” east longitude.

Bounvaries.—The District of Godlpdér4 is bounded on the 
north by the Bhutdn Hills; on the east by the rivers Mands and 
Singr4, which separate it from the District of K4mrip, and by the 
Géro Hills; on the south by the Garo Hulls; and on the west by 
the Sankos river, which separates it from the Bengal District of 
Jalpdigurt, by the Tributary State of Kuch Behar, and the Bengal 
District of Rangpur. A revenue survey of Godlpard Proper was 
carried out between the years 1849 and 1854; and of the Eastern 
Dwéis, which had then been recently annexed to the District from 
Bhutdn, in 1868 and 1869, A second revenue survey of the per- 
manently settled portion of the District was effected in 1874 and 
1875. The maps of this survey have not yet been issued, but are 
under preparation.

JuRispiction,—The District has undergone many conflicting

VOL. IL. B

18 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF GOALPARA.

changes in its jurisdiction. It originally formed a portion of 
Rangpur, and was not erected into a separate District until 13225 
at which time it comprised the police circles (4hdénds) of Godlpara, 
Dhubri, and Kardibarf. It was found necessary to exempt the 
Gd4ro mountaineers, and other rude tribes on the north-eastern 
frontier of Rangpur, from the operation of the existing Regulations, 
and to establish a special system of government for the country 
occupied by them or bordering on their possessions. This was 
done with a view to civilise the backward races, to check the 
disturbances which occurred among them, and to prevent encroach- 
ments on the part of the neighbouring landholders. The Govemor- 
General of India in Council accordingly passed Regulation x. of 
1822, by which Godlp4r4, as comprised in the three police circles 
above mentioned, was formed into a separate District, a Com- 
missioner being appointed for the administration of justice in all 
matters, The office of Deputy-Commissioner, or as he was then 
styled, Principal Assistant-Commissioner, was first established in 
February 1825. After the conquest of Assam by the British 
Government, the administration of Godlp4r4 was placed in the hands 
of the Commissioner and the Judicial Commissioner of Assam. 
Civil and criminal justice was administered according to the rules 
laid down in the Assam Code; while in revenue matters the general 
Regulations were followed, Godlpdér4 lying within that portion of 
Bengal which is included in the Permanent Settlement. The 
Assam Code was virtually repealed by the passing of the Civil and 
Criminal Procedure Codes, and since then justice has been ad- 
nunistered in accordance with these Codes, By the provisions of 
a Government notification, dated 3d December 1866, Godlpdra was 
separated from the Province of Assam, and placed under the 
Commissioner of the Kuch Behar Division in all matters. The 
Eastern Dwars, forming portion of the tract of country annexed frora 
Bhutdn at the close of the war of 1864, were at the same time 
incorporated with Godlp4r4. This arrangement, however, was not 
found convenient; and by another notification, dated roth August 
1868, the civil and criminal jurisdiction was transferred back again 
to the Judicial Commissioner of Assam. In February 1874, a fresh 
change took place, the District being incorporated with the newly 
formed Chief-Commissionership of Assam, and it now forms an 
integral part of that Province. There is no difference between the 
Magisterial and Revenue Jurisdictions, The Deputy-Commssioner

RIVER SYSTEM. 2%

putra. ‘The bifurcation takes place a little below Simldbéri, in 
Ghurld gargand. The main stream takes a south-westerly course, 
being known as the Gangddhar, ‘and empties itself into the Brahma- 
putra at Pakiméri, after running a course of forty miles through the 
District. The other and smaller branch flows to the south-east, and 
empties itself into the Brahmaputra at the Subdivisional Station of 
Dhubri. This branch, which retains the name of the parent stream, 
is now nearly dry, being only supported by the water of a small hill 
Stream, the Bdmndi. (4) The Sankos, or as it was called in ancient 
times, the Suvarnakos river, probably from gold dust being washed 
dovh by the stream, rises in the Bhut4n Hills, and after passing 
through the Eastern Dwdrs enters Godlpdr4 Proper near-Kuch Behar, 
and falls into the Brahmaputra after running a course through the 
District of thirty-two miles. In the lower part of its course, the 
channel of this river is scarcely to-be distinguished from that of 
the Gadddhar.

The following minor rivers are navigable by boats of fifty mannds 
or two tons burden, and upwards, during the rainy season :~-(1) The 
Chdémpdmati, which takes its rise in the Bhutdén Hills, and after 
passing through the centre of the Eastern Dwars and Godlpdard, falls 
into the Brahmaputra below Bildsupdrd. (2) The Kalddarni rises 
in the G4ro Hills, and falls into the Brahmaputra in the east of the 
District. (3) The Jingirdm also rises in the Gdro Hills, and empties 
liself into the Brahmaputra. (4) The Dudhndi nises in the Garo 
Hills, and falls into the Brahmaputra a few miles above the town of 
Godlp4ré ; length in the District, about eighteen miles. (5) The 
Krishndi also rises in the Gdro Hills, and empties itself into the 
Brahmaputra on its south bank near Godlpdr4; length in the District, 
twenty-two miles. (6) The Haripdni or Hathbatid river rises in 
the SdAlmdrd range of hills, and after passing through a portion of 
Khunt4éghat fargand, falls into the Brahmaputra on its north bank 
opposite Godlp4ré. (7) The Jindrf rises in the Gdro Hills, and falls 
into the Brahmaputra on its south bank a few miles above Godlpdrd. 
(8) The Tipkdi rises in the Bhutén Hills, and falls into the Brahma- 
putra after running a course through the District of about twenty- 
five miles. (9) The Bamndi river also rises in the Bhutén Hills, and 
falls into the Gadddhar near its point of bifurcation. These smaller 
streams are all fordable during the dry season, with the exception of 
the two first named

Altilough the Brahmaputra does not appear to have permanently

22 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF GOALPARA.

changed its course of late years, yet from the numerous alluvial 
accretions, sandbanks, and islands situated in its channel, there,1S 
no déubt that great changes must have taken place. There are 
places on the banks of the river, as well as at some distance from 
If, from the appearance of which it is evident that in days gone by 
the river ran in ditferent channels from the present. The banks of 
the rivers are, generally speaking, alternately abrupt and sloping; 
the beds are sandy. ‘They are not affected by the tide, nor have 
they any bore.

Lakes, Marsues, ETc.—The most important lake or di/ in the 
District is the TAmrdugé 62 in Khunta4ghdt pargand ; it is of ue 
siderable depth, and covers an area of about seven square miles. 
The Upad lake is next in importance; it is situated in Habréghat 
fargand, and covers an area of about twelve square miles. The 
only other inland sheet of water of any size is the Sdrag de in 
Parbatjodr gargand, with an estimated area of six square miles. 
Ths remaining smaller marshes, with their estimated area, are 45 
follow :—.Jaligaur, area two square miles; Kumrid, one mile; 
Bakdul, one mile; Digaldubi, three-quarters of a mile: Kishid, one 
and u quarter miles ; Padmapérd, three-quarters of a mile; Kadam- 
tald, one mile; Hasild4, one mile. .The last-named swamp is 
marsh is situated within a mile of the town of Godlpdrd4, which it 
frequently inundates to a considerable extent in the height of the 
rains. ‘I'he averagé annual loss of life in the District from drowning 
18 returned at abott ninety-six deaths; this, however, only repre 
Sents the number of cases reported to the police, and the actual 
loss of life from this cause is probably much greater.

Useg To wHIcH THE WareR 15 PuT.—The river Brahmaputra 
forms the great natural highway between Bengal and Assam. On 
an average, four steamers run from Calcutta to Assam eyery month, 
laden with goods of every description. -A very Pnteable traffic, 
both in goods and passengers, is also carried on by means of countly 
boats upon the Brahmaputra and other large rivers. With the ex- 
ception of Godlpdrd itself, there are no river-side towns in the 
District which contain a large community living by river traffic. 
None of the rivers or streams are anywhere applied as a motive 
power for turning mills or tnachinery, nor do any of them possess 
any descents or rapids which would render it likely that they could 
be so utilised by the construction of dams or weirs. The water of 
the rivers is not generally used for purposes of irrigation ; Dut the

MARSH CULTIVATION. 23

Mechs and other wild, half-civilised tribes who inhabit the borders 
of the hill country, now and then dam up the small hill streams for 
this purpose, and carry the water to their fields by means of Small 
artificial channels. There are no fishing towns, properly so called, 
in Godlp4ré District, although there are many very valuable fisheries, 
the proprietary right of which belongs to Government. and which 
are annually farmed out to the highest bidders. A considerable 
proportion of the inhabitants gain a subsistence by fshing; and the 
produce of the fisheries, besides furnishing the necessities of the 
District, also supplies the Gdros and Bhutids with dried fish. On 
every market day in the little villages on the Gdro Hills frontier. a 
large quantity of dried fish is either sold for cash or bartered for 
cotton, cloth, and other commodities.

MarsH CULTIVATION.—No rivers or marshes have anywhere been 
embanked with a view to the extension of cultivation, etc., nor have 
they been utilised as reed or cane producing grounds. It is most 
probable, however, that they are capable of being so utilised, as 
reeds and canes grow spontaneously. Besides the inhabitants of 
Godalp4rd, large numbers of people regularly come up from the neigh 
bouring Districts of Rangpur and Maimansinh to gather the canes and 
reeds which grow abundantly on the sandbanks, river-sides, and in 
the numerous marshes and swamps of the District. Long stemmed 
rice is extensively cultivated in Godlp4rd, chiefly of the following 
kinds :—Zewé-bdo, which grows in from eighteen to twenty-one feet 
of water, the growth of the stem keeping pace with the rise of the 
floods ; any very sudden inundation or rise of water, however, would 
overtop the plant and destroy it; 7d/-ddéo is cultivated in from fifteen 
to eighteen feet of water; sav-matt, in from twelve to fourteen feet ; 
mera-béo, in from nine to ten and a half feet; and 2ekoyd-bdo, in from 
three to four and a half feet. Many of the marshes, as well as lands 
which are subjectéd to regular inundations, are cultivated with this 
variety of rice. No increase in the length of stem kas been effected, 
so that it can be cultivated in a greater depth of water than formerly. 
The fact that a very considerable portion of the District is under 
water for some months every year, makes the cultivation of this rice 
@ matter of great importance. The country is inundated less by 
the numerous small hill and other streams, than by the great Brahma. 
putra river, which annually floods a large tract of land on both sides 
of its banks. Thiseriver in the flood season rises above the level 
of the’mouths of the minor streams, and forces their waters back

24 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF GOALPARA.

in an opposite direction to that in which they naturally flow, thus 
cauging an inundation of the low-lying tracts. The carrying out 
of the long contemplated Trunk Road along the south bank of the 
Brahmaputra, and of a similar work along the northern bank, with 
Strong bridges and aqueducts for the passage of the water, would 
greatly contribute to relieve the country from the effects of inunda- 
tions, which at present are often very destructive.

MINERALS; ButLpinc StonE, ETC.—No discoveries of coal 
mines, stone quarries, or minerals, have been made in Godipara 
District. The hills abound with large heavy stones, which, it is 
believed, might be utilised for building purposes. That this’ was 
done by the ancient native kings is evident from the Thdkeswarl 
temple in this District, besides numerous temples in K4mnip and 
other parts of Assam, constructed of these stones. No caverns, hot 
springs, or interesting natural phenomena have yet been discovered 
in Godlpérd,

ForEsts.—Several large and important forests exist in the Eastern 
Dwérs, as well as in Godlp4rd Proper, which yield good profit ; and 
the sdéZ timber trade of the Dwaérs, and of Parbatjodr and other 
Pargands, has contributed considerably to the wealth and prosperity 
of the District. An important source of income to the landholders 
of Parbatjodr, and in a minor degree to several other landlords, 15 
derived from taxes or cesses termed gor kati (felling timber) and 
thadjat (stacking timber). Their total income under these two 
headings is estimated at about #3000 per annum. Under recent 
mS ougements, the felling of sd? timber (Shorea robusta) in the 
Eastern Dwars, in Government estates, and in the estate of the minor 
Raja of Bijnf, which is under the management of the Court of 
Wards, has becn prohibited, Arrangements are now being made 
in the Department of the Conservator of Forests for the preserva- 
ton of the sd? trees in the Eastern Dwérs. Besides sd/, the forests 
contain the following useful timber trees :—Gambhéri (Gmelina 
arborea), used for boat-building ; pamd (Cedrela toona), for making 
canoes and furniture; chémd, for boat-building and general pur- 
Poses ; shélekhd (Cetrena) ; wridm (Andrachne trifoliata) ; jasmbora 
adam (Nauclea kadamba) ; stxgari (Castanea) ; odd/ (Stercuha 
urens) ; gandh sarat (Camphora glandulifera); dargdchh (Ficus 
elastica) ; simu (Bombax Malabaricum). These, together with 
ifhumerable other kinds of trees, bamboos, cants, etc., comprise the 
heavy forests and jungles of Godlpér4 , District. Those proprietors

FORESTS. 25

whose land is situated in Godlpdr4 Proper do not pay any revenue 
to Government for their forests, which are included as an integral 
part of their permanently segtled estates.

As regards the Government forests, the following paragraphs 
are condensed from the Progress Report on Forest Administration 
in Assam for 1874-75. A general inspection and survey of the 
forests of this Province, then ineluded within Bengal, was con- 
ducted in 1869-70, prior to which year there had been no con- 
servancy or realisation of revenue proper from the Government 
forests. ‘In the valuable sdéZ forests of the Eastern Dwars, in Godl- 
par® District, the Bengal woodcutters were found exceedingly busy, 
cutting down whatever they wanted, and a great deal more, as was 
evident from the number of partly used trees lying on the ground. 
They only paid Rs. 4. 4. o (8s. 6d.) per axe for a year, which was 
calculated to be at the rate of R. o. 2. 8 (4d.) per tree, according 
to the timber-cutters’ own statement, that each man brought down 
fifty logs, and supposing two logs to be cut out of one tree, which 
is a very high estimate. At this time, logs realised on the Brahma- 
putra Rs. 10 to Rs. 15 (A1 to £1, ros. od.) per pair. Wood- 
cutters were in no way restricted as to size or quantity, and acted 
entirely as sulted their own interests.’ From 1870-71 the early 
selection and demarcation of the better forests as reserves was 
ordered to be placed in charge of the Forest Department ; while 
the remainder were to be managed by the Deputy-Commissioners, 
to each of whom was attached a forest establishment, at a cost for 
every*District of Rs. 72 per month, or £86, 8s. od. a year.

The forest area in Godlp4r4 District is roughly estimated at 430 
square miles, being 9 per cent. of the forest area of the entire Province 
of Assam within revenue limits, and ro per cent. of the total area of 
the District. This figure does not include the extensive forest areas, 
where jz cultivation is allowed; nor the grass savannahs covered 
with trees, which abound in Assam. But the Forest Divisions in 
Assam do not uniformly correspond with the ordinary executive 
Districts; and the Godlp4r4 Division wil ultimately include what- 
€ver forests may hereafter be set apart in the Gdro Hills. At 
present it comprises the Government open forest in the Eastern 
Dwars, which has an area of 422 square miles. Of this tract about 
eighty square miles are sd/ forest; the contents of which are estimated 
at two and a half nfillions of sd/ trees, with an annual yield, if pro- 
perly protected, of 25,000 trees, This is the most valuable Govern-

26 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF GOALPARA.

ment property in the Province, and was declared ‘Government open 
forest’ from July 1872, under Act vii. of 1865. But from its ac- 
cessible position the mature timber has gll been worked out, and no 
large income can be expected for some years to come, Except sd/ 
(Shorea robusta), sissw (Dalbergia sissoo), Zhayer (Acacia catechu), 
and chelauni (Schima vel Gordonia mollis), all other timber is free 
in an ‘open forest.’ In the year 1873-74, the expenditure on the 
Forest Department in the GodlpdrA Division was Rs. 937 (£93) 
148. od.); while the income, excluding the receipts of the civil 
officers, was Rs. 169 (£15, 18s. od.). A proposal 1s now (1875) 
under the consideration of the Chief Commissioner, by which*the 
forests in the Bijn{, Chirang, R{ipu, and Gumd4 Dwars shall be de- 
clared forest reserves, and thus brought under more stringent rules 
of conservancy. As regards the Sidli Dwér, in which the forests are 
at least equally valuable, it will be necessary to make a preliminary 
examination and survey. ‘The great danger’ to which the forests 
in the Eastern Dwdrs are exposed, is the spread of zim cultivation, 
which is jealously guarded against as regards areas on which sal, 
sissu, khayer, and chelauni trees are growing. In 1874-75 two cases 
of forest trespass were tried, in which five persons were concerned, 
all of whom were convicted.” The present area gazetted a5 un 
reserved forest in Bijnf Dwér is 12°56 square miles, or 3 per cent. 
of the total area; in Sidl{ Dwdr, 74°37 square miles, or 21 per cent. 
of the total area; in Chirang Dwér, 245°76 square miles, Gr 5° 
per cent. of the total area; in Ripu Dwér, 6s square miles, of 27 
per cent. of the total area ; in Gumd Dwar, 24°6 5 square mile#or 26 
per ‘cent. of the total area; total, 422 square miles, or about 25 
per cent. of the aggregate area of the Eastern Dwéts. In addition, 
there are about eight square miles of unreserved forest in Godlp4r4 
Proper.

JUNGLE Propucts; Pasture Lanps, erc.—The chief jungle 
products of the District are beeswax and dyes; but it is supposed 
that fibres are to be found, and that these will form an article of 
trade. A considerable number of native medicinal drugs are also 
found among the wild vegetable products, but it is not known 
whether there are any that are made use of in the pharmacopceia of 
Europe. The castes and hill tribes who gain their subsistence by 
collecting and trading in jungle products and timber are the Mech, 
Cachari, Rébh4, Gdro, Réjbans{, and Hajong. ~About six hundred 
boats come up every year from Sirdjganj, Dacca, and other” places

POPULATION. 27

in Bengal, for the purpose of purchasing timber. It is estimated 
that they carry down with them timber to the average value of £20 
for each boat. The total value of the timber traffic would thus 
amount to about £12,000 per annum. Most of the small islands 
formed by the Brahmaputia, as well as certain other localities in the 
District, are used as pasture lands for large herds of cattle which are 
annuaily brought up from other parts of Bengal. These patches 
of pasture land are scattered ajl over the District, and their area 
has never been ascertained. The landowners levy a tax from the 
owners of the herds, called Ad/dchardz, which is estimated to yield 
a tofal gum of, about £200 per annum.

Fer& Natur#®.—The large sorts of game common in Godlpdr4 
are tigers, leopards, rhinoceros, bears, buffaloes, and deer. Rewards 
are offered by Government for the destruction of wild animals, the 
amount so disbursed being equal to an average of £139, 135. od. 
per annum, for the three years ending in 1870. It was subse- 
quently found necessary to increase the rates of reward offered for 
the killing of wild animals. Thc Deputy-Commissioner states, on 
the authority of the District records, that, about twenty-five years 
ago, more money was paid in one year for killing wild animals than 
was realized from the land revenue. The number of deaths from wild 
beasts and snake bites, during the same three years mentioned above, 
averaged 116 aycar. No rewards have ever been given for snake- 
killing in Godlpdra. The smaller sorts of game consist principally 
of partridges, pea-fowl, jungle-fowl, floricans, quail, wild duck, etc. 
Crocodiles abound in the different rivers. There is no regular 
trade in wild-beast skins, but the skins of tigers which have been 
killed for the sake of the Government reward are sold for small 
sums.

ETHNICAL DIVISION OF THE PEOPLE.~-Godlpdrd, as being the 
frontier District of Assam, and having been often included within 
the political limits of Bengal, naturally presents mixed ethnological 
features. Excluding the population of the Eastern Dwdrs, the 
Census of 1872 returns 86,001 Hindus proper, or 21‘o8 per cent. of 
the entire population. -The Muhamimadans of Goalpdrd, for the 
most part of the same ethnical origin, number 89,916, or 22°05 per 
cent., being more than one-half of the entire Musaimdn population 
in all Assam. The semi-Hinduized aborigines are 132,095 in 
number, or 32°89 per cent. of the total; and among them the Kochs 
are especially numerous. The aboriginal tribes number 97,732, or 
23°99 per cent., chiefly made up of R4bhds, Mechs, Cdchéris, and 
Garos. Both the Mechs and the Gdros of Assam appear to be 
absolutely confined to Godlpard District; which also contains just 
ofte-half of the total Rébhds to be found in the Province.

The following list is taken from the District Census Compilation

cation differs in some minor points from that adopted in the 
Census Report. Amongst other changes, the total of the Kochs 
has been considerably reduced by the exclusion of a number of 
Musalmdn Kochs, who had been erroneously included; and the

GAURIPUR. 49

Brahmaputra, in north Jatitudé 26° to’ 25", ahd east longitude go° 
40° 50”. It has always been the principal seat of commerce in the 
Districts although, with its suburbs, it only contains a population, 
according to the census of 1872, of 4678 souls: So long ago as 
1788, the name of a Mr. Raush appears as a merchant setiled here, 
who was in a position to send a force of 7oo armed guards to fight 
the Modmdrids who had rebelled against the Assam king. In the 
time of the Assam Réjds, a guard was stationed at Hadird, opposite 
Godlpdré, which interfered with the free access of trading boats 
into Assam from Bengal. In consequence of this, merchants 
and treders used to settle at the town of Godlpdrd, or at Jogigaphd, 
on the opposite side of the river. In addition to the ordinary 
local traffic, the products of the Gdrod Hills on the south, and of 
the Bhutan Hilis on the north, are brought to Godlpard, and meet 
with a ready sale. The Civil Station is built on an oblong ml, 
258 feet high, and upwards of. 600 feet above sea-level. ‘There 
are a few privatevhouses on the hill, occupied by the officials 
of the District, and the missionary. On the summit is a fine 
piece of table-land, commanding a splendid view of the Bhutén 
and Himédlayan ranges, and also of the Gdro Hulls. At almost all 
seasons of the year there.is a breeze blowing, and the only objec- 
tion to the spot is the fatiguing ascent, which is too steep for 
carriages. The native town of Godlpdrda is situated on the western 
side of the hill. It is dry and regularly built, consisting of several 
large streets running parailel to each other, with cross roads at right 
angles. In the main street—the one nearest the river—are ail the 
shops ; and the whole town, with.the exception of a few masonry dwell- 
ings, is constructed of wooden posts, bamboo mats, and thatching grass. 
Fires are éonsequently .of frequent occurrence, particularly during 
the hot months of March, April, and May. There is a good sized 
market at the top of the main street, whith is in general welt stocked 
with fish, fowls, and native vegetables, but no animal food is procur- 
able, The immediate vicinity of the town consists of plain land, a 
good portion of which is "under cultivation, intersected with small 
hills and large marshes. During the height of the rains, portions of 
the towh are inundated, and in some parts the people have to us¢ 
canoes to proceed from their houses to the raised roads. 
GauRipuR.—The next town in importance is Gauripur, on the 
north bank of the Brahmaputra, in Gold Alamginj pargandé, which 
contains «he residence of the richest Jandholder ot the District, and

TEMPLES AND PLACES OF PILGRIMAGE. —THere i is a famous temple 
on the top of a hill in Habrdghdt Fiscal Division, dedicated to the 
Godde&s Durgd. The building is called the Thdkeswarf Temple, 
and its architecture proves the engineering ‘skill of the person who 
designed and built it. People from all parts of India, especially 
Sanydsis or 1ehgious mendicants, make pilgrimages to the place. 
The hillgs tenanted by monkeys; and the two chief ones, a male and 
female, are said to be the king’and queen, and, as such, are con- 
sidered sacred by the pilgrims. Another place of Hindu pilgrimage 
is the temple of Dudh-ndth, sacred to Siva, at Jogigophd, on the 
north bank of the Brahmaputra, opposite Godlp4té. There are several 
artificial caverns, or holes cut in the rocks, found m the ncighbour- 
ing hills, which are said to have been constructed by olden sages for 
the purposes of devotion and meditation ; and their position and 
the surrounding scenery seem to favour this supposition.

MatTERiAL CONDITION OF THE PEOPLE.—The Mechs, Cachdris, 
Rdbh4s, Gdros, and most of the hill people inhabiting the District, 
are very simple in their manners and habits. Necessity or revenge 
seems to be the common cause of their pffences against thé criminal 
law.’-. Théy commit thefts arfd*even murders ; but in the majority of 
cases they confess their guilt as soon as they are apprehended. The 
people of Godlpara generally dre good imitators, and anything that is’ 
new to them they try to copy. Thig is partly owing tq the fact’ that 
caste restrictions are not so strict as they are in other parts of Bengal. 
Ties of relationship are highly regarded, and appear to be very 
binding among them; even distant relatives are acknowledged to 
have the cght of the shelter of a home in case of want or necessity. 
The Céch4fis and other hill tribes drink a liquor prepared by them- 
Selves from rice, and a large proportion of the people either eat or 
smoke opium., Many of them;also smoke gdajd, an intoxicating 
preparation ‘of hemp. European wines and spirits have been intro- 
duced into the Station, and.it is a matter of regret that some of the

52 STATISTICAL AGCOUNT OF GOALPARA.

country people have begun to use them. Education does not seem 
to “ave hitherto, made much progress among the people. In 187), 
the Deputy-Commissioner stated that he was of opinion that not 
more than eight hurldred boys had received instruction at the few 
schools in the District. The only schoo! in Godlpdrd for girls is 
one established by the American Baptist Mission, and there is ne 
institution for giving instruction in any of the fine or useful arts or 
sciences. There is a society called the Hité Bidhd4yini Sabhd, the 
members of which conduct a school with the aid of a grant from 
Government, and have raised subscriptions for the purpose of estab- 
lishing a printing press in the District. The landholders, who derive 
a Jarge income from the people, have paid very little attention towards 
tne promotion of education among them ; and the Deputy-Commis- 
sioner is of opinion that the time is still far distant when there will 
be a genetal diffusion of knowledge among the mdsses. The people 
are in prosperous circumstances, as a general rule; they are very 
independent, and decline to work as coolies or day-labourers. In 
consequence of this, it is very difficult for the public officers to 
obtain labourers in times when urgently wanted, unless the Jand- 
holders or others are directed to supply them, or assist in procuring 
them. It is almost impossible also to obtain domestic servants, 
and those that do engage themselves as such, will not work unless 
they are paid excessive wages. ) 
Dress, Foop, Buitpincs, erc.—The ordinary dress of a well- 
to-do shopkeeper consists of a cloth worn round the waist, and 
reaching as far as the knees (dhutt), a cotton sheet or shawl 
(chddar), and now and then a coat (pirdn). The clothing of a 
common péasant is simply a waistcloth and a cotton shawl.. The 
materials used in the construction cf the buildings of all classes 
consist ‘of bamboo, grass, wood, canes, jute rope and reeds; mats 
are how being used as walls for houses. The residence of a well- 
to-do shopkeeper Zeneraily consists of an outer apartment, which 
forms his shop or place of business, with sometimes an inside 
room, with a platforra or machdén for keeping stores. He has also 
generally another house within the same enclosure, consisting of 
two, and in some cases of three rooms. In this houtfe the shop- 
keeper dwells with his family, the other members of his household 
occupying. the shop at night. ‘Both these houses are surrounded by 
an outer wall or enclosure. The part of the house where the shop- 
keeper transacts his business is constructed on a raised platform,

DRESS, FOOD, BUILDINGS, ETC. 53

and covered with mats. A portion of it is covered by a thick 
Striped cotton carpet (satranji), or a white sheet, where the shop- 
keeper sits, and on which isa large pillow, and a wooden box to 
keep his books, papers, etc. in. Two hookahs, one for Bréhmans 
and the other for Siidras, with stands, and two or three stools, far 
the accommodation of visitors or persons who have come on 
business, are also generally placed on the platform. Another small 
box is used,asacash-box. Two or three paper paintings of Ganesh, 
Kali, or some other of the Hindu deities Adorn the wall, on which 
are also hung one or two letter files.- In the,cook-house, a platform 
is rais€d over the hearth for the purpose of stowing firewood and 
other articles used for culinary purposes. In the sleeping-room 
there is a lamp and lampstand, a seat or two, a few mats, and a 
large box to deposit cash, ornaments, clothing, etc. in. The master 
of the house sleeps either on:a wooden cot, or on a bamboo plat- 
form- An ordinary husbandman generally has two houses, one 
of two rooms, in which he keeps his stores gr other requisites ; 
and tne other of threé rooms, in which he cooks his food, eats, and 
sleeps. In a few cases, a separate hut is built for the cattle, but 
otherwise one of the rooms of the storehouse is given up as a 
cowshed. A few mats, seats, and a wooden lamp tompose nearly 
‘alhis furniture. The different houses which compose his dwelling 
are all surrounded by an enclosure. The ordinary food of the 
family of a well-to-do shopkeeper consists of rice, split peas, fish, 
vegetables, and milk. The Deputy-Commissioner estimates that the 
living expenses of a middling-sized household, consisting, say, of the 
following members, viz. father, mother, brother, brother’s wife, and 
young child, two sons and a daughter, and old widow aunt, or other 
relative of the master, two men-servants, and a maid-servant, would 
amount to about Rs. 25 or £2, ros. od. per month. In this case, 
however, the milk would be supplied by the cows of the fginily, and 
the firewood gathered from the jungle without coSt by the servants. 
The living expenses of an ordinary husbandman, with a household 
consisting of six persons, would not amount to upwards of Rs. 11.8.0 
or £1, 3s. od. per mensem. This would,represent the cost, if he 
had to purchase everything in the bdzdr, but a great proportion of his 
necessaries he produces himself, or ‘manages to obtain withaut cost. 
Betel nut and tobacco he obtains from his garden, rice and \ egetables 
from his fields, firewood is collected from the jungle, and fish from the 
nearest marsh or river, all without any other cost than his owh labour.

AGRICULTUKE.—The, staple crop of Godlpér4 is rice, divided 
into three great classes, which again ipre subdivided into several 
different varieties. The first of the thfee classes is the dus 
or ditari rice, which is subdivided into the following principal 
varieties :—Chdmnple; nildjt, dubechengd, pakrisdbetd, bhujshd, gathit 
bhimrd, kdsté pdnjé, bodlidar, and kehkoyd. Uhe dus rice is sown 
in January, February, March, and Apuil, principally in the two latter 
months, and reaped in May, June, July, August, but principally in 
June and July. Tt requires soniewhat moist land, and 1s not 
transplanted. dv or, long-stemmed rice requires marshy land, 
and is also not transplanted. It is chiefly sown in #ebrudry and 
March, and reaped in October and November. The third class of 
rice is the haimantik, sdli, or dman crop, which demands 2 sandy; 
moist, or clayey land, so that the roots of the plants may be 
always in water. It is sown in nurseries in May, June, and July, 
transplartied in July, August, and September, especially in July and 
August, and reaped in November, December, and January. Its 
priacipal varieties are—Zulsi_jébd, khariké jubd, madhukar mdaldtt, 
malbhog, banni, kdld sudgmani, dudh sudgmani, jul bardhdn, el: jdbri, 
mainagurt, akhaia or kdtdri dibud, dal kachu, ringi bardhdn, santt 
bokd, and pérd-chakhud. The pdrd-chakhud, and one or two others 
of the above-mentioned species, are called dokdé dhén, a variety of, 
tice which is consumed uncooked. It is steeped in water-until it 
becomes soft, in which state it is eaten with curds or molasses. 
This is a favourite mid-day meal among the peasantry throughout 
Assam. The only other species of rice grown in Godlp4rd is that 
called Jkarmd, sown in nurseries in May and June, transplanted in 
June and July, and reaped in September and October. It requires 
a moist soil for its suceessful cultivation. The other celeal crops 
grown in the District are as follows :—Gohkam (wheat), sown upon 
the same description of land as dws rice, in October and November; 
and hatvested in February and March. Jab (barley) is cultivated 
only to a limited extent, in one or two places. The seasons for 
reaping and sowing, and the natue of the land, are the same as 
for wheat. But (gram) requires the same kind of land as pulse 
crops. Ii is sown in the months of October and November, and 
reaped .in February and Masch. Czind is sown in October, 
November, and December, and reaped in February, March, and 
Apul. don is planted in November and December, and reaped 
in March, April, and May. Serishd (mustard) is cultivated in great

abundance on the chars and alluvial accietions in the bed of the 
Brahmaputra, which afford excellent soil for this crop - It is also

The: following paragraphs regarding ‘the various classes of schools 
in Godlp4ré District'are eas from the Report of the Education 
Department for 1872-73 :-—

‘GOVERNMENT HicHER-CLass ENGLISH ScHooL.—The. Deputy- 
Commissioner writes : -—It is a matter of great regret that, notwith- 
standing so much money is yearly spent by Government on this 
school, its condition is anything but satisfactory. For the past five 
years there has been a steady decrease in the numerical strength 
of the school, as the following figures show :—In 1868-69 theré 
were 120 pupils on the last day of the year; in 1869-70, 100; in 
1870-71, "go; in 1873-72, 68; and in 1872-73, 57. This ee 
falling off in the number of anidents has from time to time been 
attributed to various causes, such as the indifference of parents to 
anything like real education for their boys, enhancement of fees, 
the establishment of a vernacular school in the close vicinity, and 
the like. But I fear the real cause lies beyond any of these. Since 
the conversion of the school from an aided to a Government one, 
in 1864-65, it has not passed a single candidate in the University 
entrance examination ; and if the parents show any indifference to 
keeping their boys long in the schgol, it is partly because of the 
repeated bad success of ‘the school at the University examinations. 
It is believed that in one or two instances, parents anxious to give 
their children a good English education have been compelled. to 
send them to other District schools.”

‘There are no GOVERNMENT MIDDLE- Crass ScHooLs in this 
District. |

‘AlpED MuippLeE-CLass ENGLISH ScHOOLS.—There are three 
schools of this class, with 161 pupils. These are fayourably 
reported on .by the officiating Deputy-Inspector. The Deputy- 
Commissioner visited and examined one of them, but thought there 
was much room for improvement, “both in respect to the number 
of pupils and their advancement.”

“AIDED MIppLE-CLASS VERNACULAR SCHOOLS.— There are 10 
of these schools in the District, one less than in 871-72. The

CuimmaTe.—The prevailing winds are easterly, but hot winds blow 
occasionally from the west in the months of March, April, and 
May, and during these months there are also occasional strong 
winds and heavy storms from the south-west. The monsoon com- 
mences about the 15th May, and is generally over by the middle of 
October. The cold weather sets in about November, and lasts till 
the end of February. During this season there are daily heavy 
fogs, which rise shortly after daybreak, and gradually clear off 
between 9 and 10 o’clock.

MEptcaL Aspecrs: Enpemics.—The prevailing diseases among 
the native population of Godlpdrd are intermittent and remittent 
fevers, complicated with affections of the spleen; diarrhcea, dysentery, 
cholera, rheumatism, chest alfections, and occasionally a few cases of 
small-pox. The European residents chiefly suffer from intermittent 
fever at times causing great prostration, diarrhoea, dysentery, and 
rheumatism. The Civil Station of Godlpdr4, and many other parts 
of the Déstrict, are very malarious; and the whole District is con- 
sidered, both by Europeans and natives, especially in the rainy season, 
or from May till about November, to be the most unhealthy in Assam 
or North-Eastern Bengal, Tor several miles around the Station of 
Godlpdrd, except on the north side where the river flows, there is 
nothing but flat marshy ground, the greater portion of which is 
covered with jungle. When the river rises, all this tract is converted 
into a huge swamp ; and were it not for the raised roads, it would 
be impossible to move about. Little or no attention is paid to 
bunitation in any part of the District, and the people are too poor 
to call for municipal arrangements. None of the swamps has been 
drained, nor has any of the jungle been cleared.

EprpEmics.—Cholera has repeatedly made its appearance in an 
epidemic form in the District, and numbers are said to have died 
from it ; but no detailed information can be given, as it is only very 
recently that any mortuary returns have been kept. In October 
1868 the epidemic made its appearance in the Dhubri Subdivision, 
and spread along both banks of the river. The disease did not 
wear itself out till July in the following year, and again made its ap- 
pearance on the north bank of the Brahmaputra in November. No 
information can be obtained as to the number who were attacked, 
ot as to the mortality, but it is stated that more men were attacked 
than women. The classes of people who suffered were chiefly the 
poor and ill-fed. To protect the inhabitants, native doctors were 
sent from Calcutta and the Civil Station to treat those who were 
attacked, Pills and chlorodyne were also distributed among some 
of the villages, with directions as to how the medicine was to be 
administerv-J, There are no records of any epidemic of small-poa 
01 of fever vecurring in the District. Among cattle diseases, a fatal 
Gisorder, cailea chapkd, is said to be prevalent during the rains and

704 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT Of GOALPARA.

hot weather. It begins first with sores breaking out in the mouth ; 
the hoofs then become affected, and rot ; diarrhoea afterwards sets 
in ; and the poor beast generally dies.

VitaL Sratistics have been collected for many years past 
throughout Godlpdr4, as in Bengal, through the agency of, the 
chaukidérs or village police, and not through the mausdddrs, as in 
Assam Proper. The figures obtained in this way are altogether 
untrustworthy, as may be inferred from the circumstance that the 
proportion of deaths throughout the District generally was in the 
year 1874 less than one-fourth of the proportion in the selected 
areas. From the commencement of 1873 a new systenfhas been 
introduced by the side of the old, in accordance with which more 
accurate returns, both of births and deaths, are obtained from cer- 
tain selected areas. In Godlpdrd District, the urban area chosen 
is Godlpdr4 town and suburbs, with a population of 6061 persons ; 
the rural area comprises the village of Dalgumd with its neighboui- 
hood, and has a population of 5145. In the urban area, the agency 
consists of two police constables, supervised by the dék moharrir, at 
a monthly cost of Rs. 4, or £4, 16s. per annum. In the rural area, 
two constables of KAmérpdtar outpost send in monthly returns to the 
Civil Surgeon, and each receive R. 1 a month; total cost, £2, 45. 
perannum. The returns, at least of the deaths, may be accepted as 
fairly accurate. In the year 1874 the number of deaths reported 
in the urban area was 245, Or 40°4 per thousand ; in the rural area, 
352 deaths, or 68-4 per thousand, making a ratio for the combined 
areas of 53 per thousand. The causes of death were as follow:— 
Fevers, 333; cholera, r1 3; bowel complaints, 85 ; small-pox, 10; 
Snake-bite and wild beasts, 7 ; accidents, 2; suicide, 1; all pther 
causes, 46. The births registered in the same year were—in the 
urban area, 62 males and 64 females; total, 126, or 20°7 per 
thousand: in the rural area, 109 males and 93 females; total, 202, 
or 39°2 per thousand ; making a ratio for the combined areas of 
29°27 per thousand.

Godlp4rd town has been attended to so far as available funds admit ;

travellers, have been placed at ‘ntervals along the road from, Kuch

the water of which is used for drinking purposes.’

INDIGENous Drucs.—The principal vegetable medicines found 
in the District are the following :—(1) Areca catechu, very com- 
mon; (2) Caunabis Indica, found wild in many parts; (3) Czesal- 
pinia, a creeper said to be used by the people in fevers; (4) 
Calatropis gigantea, very common, and used by the native prac- 
titioners as a purgative and emetic ;*(;) Croton tigliura, common 
all over the District; (6) Gynocardia odorata, found, but not 
common ; (7) Ricinus communis, common in many parts of the 
Nisirict. No mineral drugs are found. Little or nothing is known 
of the medicines used by the native practitioners, as they keep them 
secret. They generally administer their medicines in the form of

a powder mixed with honey o1 water, and also make use of charms, 
and incantations,

STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF’ THE 
EASTERN DWARS.

—>—

eh ae EASTERN DWARS, which now form the northern

portion of Godlpdra District, are situated between 26° 54 
2” and 26° 17’ 47" north latitude, and go® 39' 56” and 89° 48 23" 
east longitude. They contain an area of 1568 square miles, and a 
population of 37,047, according to a Census taken by the Deputy- 
Commissioner in 1870. ‘There are no towns in the Dwars, but the 
principal village is Bijni, situated on the north bank of the Dalani 
river, in latitude 26° 29 25" N. and longitude 90° 45’ 46" E. The 
Dwérs are administered from Godlpdra town, the Civil headquarters 
of the entire District.

BounvarRiges.—The Eastern Dwars are bounded on the north by 
the Independent State of Bhutan; on the east by the Mands and 
Dhirsuti rivers, which separate them from the District of Kamrup ; 
on the south by Godlpdéra Proper ; and on the west by the Sankos 
river, also called the Gadadhar farther down, which separates them 
from the Western Dwars, now part of the Bengal District of J alpdi- 
guri, and from the State of Kuch Behar.

JurtspicTion.—When the Bhutén Dwérs, or the level strip of 
country at the foot of the Bhutén Hills, were annexed by the British 
at the end of 1864, they were divided into two parts, the Eastern and 
Western Dwars. That part of the country lying between the Manas 
and Sankos rivers was formed into an independent District named 
the Eastern Dwars, and placed in charge of a Deputy-Commissioner, 
who -had his headquarters at Datm4 in the Khuntaghat Fiscal 
Division of Godlpdérd. This arrangement did not continue for long. 
in December 1866 the Eastern Dwars were added to Godlpard, 
and now form the most northern part of that District. ~

All suits relating to immoveable property, revenue, and’ rent 
are excepted from the jurisdiction of the Civil Courts by Act xvi. of

108 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF EASTERN DWARKS.

1869. The Eastern Dwats, as well as the whole of Godlpdr4 Dis- 
ixict, are administered by a Deputy-Commissioner, an cxtra Assist- 
ant-Commissioner, and a subordinate Civil Judge stationed at Godl- 
par4, and an extra Assistant-Commissioner and a subordinate Civil 
Judge at the Subdivisional Station of Dhubri. Godlpdrd Proper 
was originally comprised within Bengal, and formed a portion of the 
territories which were included in the Permanent Settlement. On 
the conquest of Assam, the administration of Godlp4ré was vested 
in the Commissioner of that Province. Upon the formation of the 
Kuch Behar Division, Godlpd4r4 was separated altogether from 
Assain, and placed under the Commissioner of Kuch Behar. This, 
however, was found inconvenient; and subsequently the criminal 
jurisdiction of the District was transferred back again from the 
Commissioner of Kuch Behar to the Judiciaf Commissioner of 
Assam, the Revenue and Executive Departments remaining as 
before under the Commissioner of Kuch Behar. Finally, in 1872, 
the Eastern Dwéars, together with the whole of Godlpdrd Distaict, 
were transferred to the Chief-Commissionership of Assam. 
GENERAL ASPECT OF THE CouNTRY.—The Eastern Dwars form a 
flat strip of country, lying beneath the foot of the Bhutdn range of 
mountains. The chief characteristics are the numerous rivers and 
hill streams which intersect it in every diection, and also the large 
tracts of sd/ forests, and the heavy grass and reed jungies, inter- 
spersed with wild cardamums. These grass and reed jungles are 
especially dense and luxuriant along the banks of the rivers and 
streams, where the vegetation grows to a considerable height, and is 
impenetrable toman. The beautiful cotton tree (Bombax Malabari- 
cum) is to be found in these tracts, growing in large numbers with sur- 
prising vigour and rapidity, notwithstanding the annual firing of the 
jungle. With this exception, the vast tracts of grassy jungle are almost 
treeless, bringing into greater relief the sites of the villages scattered 
here and there. The villages are remarkable for the most luxuriant 
vegetation. Large clumps of bamboos and numerous plantain trees 
hem them in on every side, almost hiding the houses from view; 
while, rising above the masses of bright-green foliage, are seen 
graceful betel-nut palms, with here and there a few large trees, such 
as the mango, jack, or pipal. Round about the houses, and up to 
their, very doors, grow shrubs and creeping plants of every form and’ 
variety. In the vicinity of the villages are fertile fields of rice and 
mustard cultivation. The scenery at the foot of the mountains, at

NAVIGABLE RIVERS. 109

the gorges where the larger rivérs debouch upon the plains, is very 
grand and beautiful, especially where the Sankos river leaves the 
hills,

MOUNTAINS AND ELEVATED TRACTS.—There are no mountains in 
the Eastern Dwdrs. The only hill or elevated tract is Bhumeswar, in 
the south-eastern corner of Sidli Dwar, which is from three hundred 
to four hundred feet in height. This isa detached spur of the Gdro 
ranges, and rises abruptly out of the plain; it is a Jong, narrow hill, 
covering an area of 3437 acres. It is well clothed with trees, but 
not of any great size, and brushwood, with rocks cropying out here 
and there.* The villagers in the neighbourhoqd sometimes cultivate 
patches of cotton cu ils slopes. The soil of the hill is of the same 
red, porous nature as is found in the Gdro hills, while that of the 
plains out of which it rises is not red in the least. A small Hindu 
temple is situated on the summit. The hill.can be ascended by men 
on foot, but not by cattle or other beasts of burden.

NAVIGABLFE Rivers.—The following ure the’ eleven principal 
rivers in the Eastern Dwdrs, which are all navigable by native 
boats of 100 maunds or 4 tons burjen throughout the year, for 
a certain portion of their course, or until their beds become stony 
on nearing the hills, when rapids are net with. The Mands, indeed, 
could be navigated by steamers of light draught throughoyt the 
greater part of the year. The whole country is intersected with 
small streams in every direction, too numerous to mention, all of 
which are navigable in the rainy season by boats of two tons 
burden. The eleven larger rivers are :—The Mands, Daldn{, Pdkd- 
jani, Af, Kand-m.tkra, Chdmp4émati, Gaurdng, Saralbhangé, Gangis, 
Guyupdld, and Gadddhar, also called the Sankos in the higher 
reaches of its course.

The most important stream is the Manas, which enters the Dwars 
from the Bhutdn Hills at the extreme north-east corner, and forms 
the eastern boundary till it enters Godlpdrd Proper. The chief 
tributaries of the Mands on its west bank are the Af, Daldnf 
Sondi, Bhandarni, Barchhot, Gaburkura, Gailang, and Sidnar; its 
Eriirtenies on ‘the east bank lie within the jurisdiction of the 
Deputy-Commissioner of Kdamnrip.

The Ai flows from the Bhutdn Hills in a southerly direction 
through the C hirang and Sidlf Dwars, when it turns to the east and 
runs a rather tortuous course through Bini, till it jos the Mands 
in the south-east part of that Dwar. Its chief tributaries are—on

USES TO WHICH THE WATER IS PUT. 11t

Kaémrip ; but many years ago the main stream broke into the Dwars 
for a short distance, and placed the following villages, which were 
formerly on its right bank, on its left, viz. Gordi-mari, Pdlangbarf,’ 
Patilidaha, P4étkétd, Barbilé, Mdnikpur, Mankha&sya, Jhanbdri, 
Abddi, and Nawdgrd4m. These villages are now separated from 
Assam by the Dhirsuti, which is the name of the old bed of the 
Mands. The banks of all the rivers in the Dwérs are alternately 
abrupt and sloping. The current strikes from one side of the river 
to the other in an oblique direction; and the banks are modified 
in outline, according to the direction in which the current happens 
torun. Tfie beds of all the rivers are sandy, with the exception of 
their northern portions ; when followed upwards to the hills the beds 
become pebbly, and a little higher wp they are filled with boulders. 
‘he banks are for the most part jungly, except in the neigh- 
bourhood of villages. The Mands, Ka4ndmdkrd4, Buri Af, Ai, Sdrul- 
bhdng4, and Sankos rivers have water in their beds at the points 
where they leave the Bhutén Hills. But the channeis of all the rest 
are dry at the foot of the hills except during the rains, and the water 
only makes its appearance at a considerable distance from the hills, 
having percolated under ground to the place where it appears on 
the surface. None of the rivers anywhere expand into lakes.

Forps AND FERRIES.—There is a ferry on the Mands river where 
the road crosses it, a little below the point where it forms a junction 
with the Daldn{f; and this river is not fordable at any time of the 
year. Three ferries have been established on the Sankos at Haldi- 
bari, Bhalk4, ahd Sagunchd4rd ; the river itself can scarcely be said 
to be fordable at any time of the year below Bhalka. Ferries are 
supposed to be kept up on all the large streams, at points where the 
road which runs east and west through the Dwars crosses them, 
There are also ferries on the Af river at Bhanddrd, and on the road 
going to Sd4lmdré; and on the Daldni river just opposite Bijni. 
The A{ is fordable in places, but not everywhere, and not at all 
during the rainy season. During the height of the rains very few 
even of the smailest streams are fordable.

USES To wHICH THE WATER IS PUT.—There are no lakes, canals, 
Or artificial watercourses in the Eastern Dwdrs; but the cultivators 
artificially irrigate their land to a great extent, by cutting channels 
from the neighbouring streams on to their fields. The whole 
country is cut up in every direction by these small watercourses, 
Shere are no river-side towns in the Eastern Dwars with a large

r12, STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF EASTERN DWARS.

community living by river traffic, but a river trade is carried on 
to a certain extent,” The chief export déspatched by means of 
the rivers is sé/ timber, cut on the southern slope’ of the Bhutdan 
mountains. The timber is lashed to boats, and floated down 
the rivers irtto the Brahmaputra, from whence it is sent on in 
the same manner to Sirdjganj, Dacca, and other places. A good 
deal of river traffic is alko carried on in rice, cotton, mustard seed, 
bete'-nut, etc. The upward traffic consists mainly of Manchester 
goods, brass and copper vessels, etc. None of the non-navigable 
rivers and streams are anywhere applied as a motive power for 
turning machinery or mills, but almost every stream could be so 
turned to account by the formation of dams. There is no com- 
munity in the Eastern Dwérs who gain their living entirely by 
fishing, but almost all the cultivators engage in it toa greater OF 
less extent. The value of the fisheries is very small as yet, owing to 
the sparse population of the country, the largest being that on the 
Sankos river, which yields at present a revenue to Governinent of 
about 410 a year only. I have no materials which would enable 
me to give the amount of revenue derived from all the fisheries in 
the Eastern Dwars.

‘MarsH Cu.tivation.—No river banks or marhes have ever 
been embanked with a view to the extension of cultivatién. Thete 
is very little marsh land in the Dwaérs at all; and as good soil 1s 
plentiful in every direction, it would be a loss of money to construct 
embankments for this purpose. Neither are’ the rivers or marshes 
utilized as reed or cane-producing grounds. Reeds ‘grow spontane- 
ously in every direction, and are used for fencing in the patches of 
cultivation, and constructing the enclosures of houses and the 
gardens around them. They are also used in making the walls 
of houses, thin covering of mud being generally plastered over 
them; and in the construction of the roofs of all small houses. 
Cane is generally found growing wild along the banks of the 
rivers and streams, and could be easily cultivated if required. It 
is generally used instead of string in building the houses of the 
people. Long-stemmed marsh rice is not cultivated in the Eastern 
Dwdrs. The total number of people who live by navigation, 
fisheries, or in any way by river industries, is very small; and the 
Deputy-Commissioner of the Dwdrs doubts whether there is a single 
person who gains his living entirely in this manner.

STATISTICAL ACCOUNT

OF THE

DISTRICT OF THE GARO HILLS.

136 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF GARO HILLS.

The boundary-line between the Gdéro Hills and Godlpard4 has also 
only been recently determined. By-this line, some tracts ‘which 
were formerly included within Godlpdra are now annexed to the 
Garo Hills. The* general principle observed in laying down the. 
boundary was to#separate from Godlp4ré all the, hilly country 
inhabited by Géros, and to include it in the newly formed Gare 
Hills District. This principle has been successfully carried out.

There was for long a dispute with respect to the southern 
boundary of the District, the point at issue being whether any 
part of the G4ro Ilills was included in the Permanent Settle- 
ment of 1793 made with the Maimansinh landholders. This 
question was involved in much uncertainty. In 1846, when the 
Survey of Maimansinh was made, the Survey officers attempted to 
lay down a boundary which would include some portion of the 
hills within Maimansinh ; but the physical difficulties attending the 
task, and the opposition of the hill tribes, compelled them to 
abandon the attempt, and the northern boundary of Maimansinh. 
was provisionally drawn along the foot of the hills. The District 
and Survey officers wished that the line thus laid down “should 
be maintained as the boundary between the Géro Hills and Mai- 
mansinh. But the R4j4 of Sus4ng and some other Jandholders of 
Maimansinh objected to this, and claimed a large part of the hills 
as included in the Settlement,

In March 1861 the Board of Revenue reported this ontier dispute 
to the Government of Bengal, as the members of the Board were 
not unanimous in the view they took of the matter. One of the 
members considered that the objections of the lancholders deserved 
attention, and that a special Commissioner should be appointed as 
arbitrator on the part of Government, to determine whether the hill 
‘racts claimed by the landholders were included or not in the Per- 
manent Settlement. The other two members thought it unneces- 
sary that any such appointment should be made, and recommended 
that the Survey line at the foot of the hills should be maintained 
as the District boundary, leaving the landholders to prosecute in a 
court of law any claim which they might be able to’ establish. The 
Government of Bengal assented to the view taken by the majority 
of the Board; but it does not appear that any action was taken in 
the matter until the 2oth August 1866, when it was notified in the 
Colcutta Gazette that the boundary should be the Jine along the foot 
of the Garo Hills as laid down by the Revenue Survey officer. and

(4) THE Netra also takes its rise on the southern side of the 
Tura watershed, and flows a verv winding course, but generally in a 
southerly direction, past the villages of Rangon, Budugizi, Gorongithi, 
Phdpa, Dasing, Gdnchung, Adipgirf, and Bogdjhordgirf, until it 
leaves the hills at Safurkot or Ghoshgd4on in Maimansinh, in which 
District it empties itself into the Kdnks river. It has no tributaries 
of any importance.

(5) Tre Sameswarl, or, as it is called by the Gdros, the Samsdng, 
is the largest stream in the District. It drains the country to 
the north of the Turd range, up to the watershed of the Ambldi 
tiver. It takes its rise on the northern face of the Turd range, neai 
the Station of Turd. The river follows/a very winding course, 
first flowing in an easterly direction for about thirty iniles, when it 
takes a turn tothe south and runs generally in a southerly direction 
till 1t emerges on the plains in pavgand Susding of Maimansinh. In 
its route it passes the villages of Semsdngixf, Dhobdékhdl, Rambdngiri, 
Danongiri, Jankdrdi, Siju, Rdyak, Parkaltham, and Awdkphang. 
Owing to rapids and granite rocks in its lower course, it is only 
navigable as far as Siju, about twenty miles from the point where it 
leaves the hills, At Jankdrdi the river flows over a sandstone bed, 
and’again becomes navigable hy fair-sized boats up to Dhobdkhd). 
At this place the rocks again crop up, and impede the navigation 
as far-as the willage of Semsdngiri. From this village to Saramphang 
Adt near the village of Sdmandal, the river is navigable all the ycar 
round by canoes of about a #aund burden. The Sameswari has 
several large tributaries, the principal of which are the Rangkdi, 
Rangéi, and Chibok, but none of them art navigable.

No important changes of course, nor any instances of alluvion 
or diluvion, are known to have taken place in the rivers. Their 
banks are usually steep, and in some places the streams flow 
though deep ravines, hardly accessible to man or beast. The beds 
of the zitcrs are generally rocky while within the hills. The 
banks of all the rivers in the Gdro Hulls are uncultivated, except in 
some rare instances, where the j#z cultivation has by chance been 
extended to the edge of the stream. None of the rivers in the 
hills form islands, nor do they anywhere expand into lakes. The 
Maheshkhdli river, which forms a part of the eastern boundary of 
the Digtrict, flows a subterraneous course for many miles under the 
limestone rocks which form its bed. Jt disappears not far from the 
¥mdka peak, and comes out again near Billar .Dhobd. AU-the

140 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF GARO HILLS.

rivers and streams within the hills are fordable at nearly every 
‘bend during the dry season, but in the rainy season many of them 
become impassable torrents. A gmall lake, or rather marsh, iS 
situated in the Gdro Hills on the summit of the Turd range near 
the village of Ranghésfgirf, on the: Nokrek hill, at an elevation of 
four thousand feet. In the rainy season it contains about thre¢ feet 
of water, but at other times it becomes dry. ‘There are no artificial 
lakes, canals, or watercourses in the Gdro Hills. Deaths by 
drowning in the District are very unusual, and probably do not 
exceed one or two cases a year, as all the rivers are very shallow, 
and are rather mountain streams than regular rivers. :

UTILIZATION OF THE WATER SupPpLy.—There is no town contain- 
ing a large community living by river traffic on the banks of any 
stream within the hills. In no case are the non-navigable rivers 
and streams applied as a motive power for turning machinery, 
although in some instances they could be made available for such a 
purpose by the construction of dams and weirs; but the Gdros are as 
yet ignorant of the simplest machinery. Nor are the waters of the 
rivers anywhere utilized for purposes of irrigation. The jum systefn 
of cultivation on the mountain slopes is the only method of 
agriculture pursued in these hills; low land or marsh rice is not 
cultivated at all. In those villages, however, situated in the low 
valleys, which were only separated in 1875-76 from Maimansinh and 
Godlpdrd, and annexed to the Gdéro Hills, and which are inhabited 
by Kochs and Hdjungs, the ordinary system of plough cultivation 
1s followed.

FisHinc.—The villages of Siju and Rayak derive considerable 
profit from fishing, and do a good trade with the Gdros of the 
inner hills by supplying them with dned fish. In the vicinity of the 
larger rivers, the people supply themselves with fish enough for their 
own consumption, but the majority of the population purchase 
this article of food in the shape of dried fish at the little village 
markets. The mode used by the Gdros in catching fish is as 
follows :—A strong dam is constructed across the hill stigams, with 
outlets for the water. Into each of these outlets is fitted a basket- 
net of split bamboo, worked into the shape of : cone. The 
water rushing through the bioader end of the bamboo cartes 
the fish with it, and finds its way out at the other end; but the 
fish are caught in the narrow part, as it is impossible for them to 
Struggle back again to the stream against the confined force of the

MINERALS. TA

warer passing through the bamboo. The Garos are generally 
ignorant of the use of nets, but they occasionally use a small one 
attached to the end of a long pole, similar to a shrimp-net in England. 
This is gradually sunk into the water, and when full the fisherman 
lifts it out with a jerk, and usually manages to capture some 
small-fry. Another mode of catching fish is frequently practised. 
The Gé4ros are acquainted with a deadly poison, which they call 
mékdl, and by means of which they poison the water; in this 
manner large numbers of fish arc killed in some of the smaller rivers. 
They are also expert divers, and kill the larger varieties of fish by 
plunging afte: them and harpooning them in the water. The diver 
arms himself with a bamboo harpoon, witha moveable barb attached 
to it by a long piece of cane. In this way large numbers of chitd/, 
and a fish called by the Gdros gi/chék, or river shark, are killed. 
The divers remain under water usually fron) a minute to a minute 
and a quarter. The Gdros also .fish with a rod and line, using 
certain kinds of insects as bait.

No rivers or marshes within the Gdro Hills have been embanked 
with a view to the extension of cultivation, nor are there any which 
present capabilities for being embanked; embankments are not 
needed anywhere within the District. The marshes produce the, 
ad/ reed, which is used elsewhere for constructing the walls of 
native huts, but is not employed in any way 2s a building material 
by the Gdros. The jungles produce abundance of canes, bamboos, 
and reeds; and as the supply is greater than the demand, there 
is no necessity for the cultivation of these products. The 
long-stemmed varicty of rice is not grown in the Gdro Hills.

Lines oF DRAINAGE,.—The drainage of the District is divided 
into two portions, the northern and southern systems, roughiy 
divided by the watershed of the ‘Turd range, which forms the back- 
bone of the District. The northern and western rivers all drain 
through Godlp4r4 District into the Brahmaputra, while those on the 
southern face of the Turd mountains flow through Maimansinh until 
they join the old channel of the Brahmaputra.

MINERALS.—CoaL has been found in several parts of the Gdro 
Hills, but it is very scarce in localities accessible by water. A good 
seam was discovered some years ago in the bed of the Mahddeo 
tiver, by Major Godwin Austen; and coal is abundant near. the 
village of Pundengru. It is also found near the village of Siju, and 
an attempt to work the mineral was made by the Raja of Susdng ;

2 STATISTEEAIF ACCOUNT OPEGLES HILLS

but it does not exist in any great quantity, and, although water- 
camiage is available by the Sameswar(, it is too far away from the 
principal centres to compete with other coal-fields. In the year 
1872-73 a find of coal near the village of Dhob4khdl, on the Sames- 
wari river, was reported by one of the Survey officers. This was 
followed up, and .ed to the discovery of large coal-fields. These 
fields have since been examined by Mr. Medlicott of the Geological 
Survey, and most favourably reported on. Fine out-crops of the 
coal are seen near the villages of Dérrangiri and Baduri, and again 
near Dhobdkhdl village. Coal is thought to exist in the country 
between Sems4ngiri and Rangrangirf, in the valley of th€ Sameswart 
river, but no qut-crops were discovered by Mr. Medlicott when he 
examined this locality in 1873-74. No traces of coal were discovered 
in the north of the District, near Jir4, where, owing to the proximity 
of the Brahmaputra river, it would have been especially valuable. 
Traces of coal were discovered, near the villages of Dipkdi and 
Andrakond4, not far from Harfgdon, but the out-crops proved of no 
value. Mr. Medlicott thought it possible that coal might be dis- 
covered at some distance below the surface in this neighbourhood, 
and it was once proposed that borings should be made; but the 
idea was abandoned, and the point still remains undecided.

LIMESTONE of a good quality, and in great abundance, has been 
discovered in the valleys of the Sameswar{ and Bhogdi rivers, and 
of an inferior quality near Turi and Damalgirf. This latter lime- 
stone was subjected to analysis in Calcutta, and was found to 
contain 79 per cent. of lime, the rest being principally clay. It 
was stated to be ‘a meagre lime, which may be used in a proportion 
not exceeding half parts of either sand or swrAi to one part of lime. 
Good building stone is found in many places in the hills. No 
metals have hitherto been discovered in the District, although there 
is little doubt that iron exists.

PICTURISQUE NATURAL PHENOMENA, ETC.—One or two caverns 
are situated in the Gdro Hills, in the limestone formation along the 
course of the Sameswarf and Ganeshwdri rivers. One of these, 
situated above the Gdro village of Rd4yak, is of considerable size. 
The entrance is about twenty feet high, and the breadth of the 
passage about fifteen feet. The cave is about a hundred feet long, 
and terminates suddenly in a high-domed charnher of considerable 
size. A small rivulet flows through the chamber, trickling out 
into the cave by a small passage too narrow to admit* a man.

Except in the Independent Gdro country, there are no valuable 
séZ forests. Considerable tracts of s’é/ forest are found near the 
villages of Bonigiri, Sokadam, Rangrangw{f, and Garigitham ; but 
these are situated in the very centre of the hills, too far away from 
water-carriage to be made much use of. ‘The lower ranges of the 
Garo Hills are well suited for the growth of; séé; and in a few years 
it ls probable that some suitable localities! near water-carriage will 
be selected and carefully reserved. But the sd/ being a very slow- 
growing tree, neither the present nor the next generation can hope 
to benefit from such measures. The existing available forests will 
continue to yield a good supply of this timber for many ‘years. The 
tree is found scattered about here and there, and the woodcutters 
take out licences from the Deputy-Commissioner to fell timber 
within certain limits. The wood is then cut into logs of six feet in 
length, and is conveyed down the nearest stream attached to canoes, 
as the timber itself will not float, to the depdt where the logs are 
Counted and taxed. The #/z tree is found, and occasionally felled. 
The revenue derived from licences for felling timber in the Gdro Hills 
amounted to £89, ts. od. in 1867-68; £40, 6s. 3d. in 1868-69; 
420, 19s. od. in 186-70; £325, 3s. od. in 1870-71; £295, 58. od. 
in 1871-72; £231, 15s. od. in 1872-73; £285, 6s. od. in 1873-74; 
and to £2005, 7s. od. in 1874-75. The collections from this source 
are most uncertain and fluctuating. In some years the woodcutters 
do not visit the hills at all, and other seasons they come in large 
numbers. The jt system of cultivation followed by the Gdros 
has been the cause of a great destruction of sé/ timber and there is 
little doubt that in former years most of the hills were covered with 
these trees, SZ is the only timber which is cut in any quantity ; 
but 4urdi and ajdr trees are occasionally felled, the former being 
a very hard, dark-coloured wood, which makes goou planks, and 
the latter a sweet-scented red wood.

183 STATISTICAL ACCOUNT OF NAGA HILLS.

While writing this Afemorandum, information has reached me 
that the Nd4gd4s have again commenced tlieir raids in the neighbour- 
hood of Nowgong, and have cut up some villagers and carried others 
away captive. Further, I know that when I was in Assam, a 
hyndred armed N4g4s came down to a plantation of the East India 
Tea Company between Gold4ghdt and Sibsdgar, and threatened the 
manager, telling him that nothing could prevent their firing his 
factory and destroying his plantation any night if they chose.’

During the progress of the Survey our parties were brought into 
collision with the Nd4gd4s. In February 1875, the Survey party in the 
eastern hills, under the charge of Lieutenant Holcombe, Assistant- 
Commissioner of Jaipur, in Sibs4gar, was attacked at Ninu, a Ndga 
village about four miles from the plains. Lieutenant Holcombe 
and eighty of the party were killed, and Captain Badgley and fifty men 
wounded. Although himself severely wounded, Captain Badgley 
effected an orderly retreat to the plains, btinging away with him his 
ainmunition and wounded. The Survey party in the western hills 
under the command of Captain Butler, the Political Officer of the 
Ndg4 Hills District, was also attacked by large parties of Ndgas 
during the night of the 4th January, and again on the roth January 
in open day; but both attacks were easily repulsed without loss. 
‘The Survey work of this party was closed when the news reached 
the Chief-Commissioner of the attack on Lieutenant Holcombe, 
and Captain Butler was ordered to join the punitive expedition 
which it had been resolved to despatch against the villages con- 
cerned in the attack. This expedition, under the command of

Colonel Nuthall, commandant of the 44th Sylhet Light Infantry, 
started early in March. The villages which were shown to have 
taken part in the massacre were attacked and destroyed. N early 
all the heads of the murdered men were recovered, and most of the 
arms and plunder that had been taken at the time of the attack 
were given up. In December 1875 only one Survey party was 
despatched into the hills, under the command of Captain Butler, 
accompanied by a small military escort. This party, a few 
Gays after entering the hills, fell into an ambuscade near the 
village of Pangti, and Captain Butler was speared by a Nd4gé con- 
cealed in the jungle through which the path lay, and died a few 
days afterwards. No other person was injured. The village of 
Pangti was destroyed next day; but the neighbouring villages re- 
mained friendly, and sent in deputations and conciliatory offerings,

THE ANGAMI NAGAS. 183

No further opposition of a serious character was met with, and tle 
Survey work viag continued, and brought to a successt{iy] conclusion. 
Besidés thei: attacks on our Survey partics, and raids on our 
villages, the Angami Nagas have continued up to the ‘present time 
in their accustomed habits of raiding and head-taking among them- 
selves. During the two ycars ending 31st March 1876, 13 separate 
outrages of this sort were reported, in which 6 villages were 
burnt and plundered, and 225 men, women, and children killed. 
These outrages continue unabated, and at the time of Writing 
(Decembgr 4877) it has been found necessary to despatch a fresh 
expedition against this tribe.

The Angdmi villages are invariably built on the very summits of 
the hi’ls, and vary much in size, some containing as many as a 
thous;nd houses, while others ‘consist of no more than twenty. 
The villages are all strongly fortified with stockades, deep ditches, 
and massive stone walls, and the hill-sides thickly studded with 
panjis, a chevaux de frise of sharp-pointed bamboo sticks planted 
in the grotind. In some casés, also, the sloping side of the hill,is cut 
away so as to form a perpendicular wall. The approaches to the 
villages are tortuous, narrow, covered ways, only wide enough to 
admit the passage of one man at a time; these lead to gates closed 
by strong, heavy wooden doors, with look-outs on which a sentry is 
posted day and night when the clans are at feud. Very often these 
approaches aré steeply scarped, and the only means of entry into 
the village is by means of a ladder consisting of a single pole, 
some fifteen or twenty feet high, cut into steps. ‘he sites of the 
villages, however, are sometimes ill chosen, being commanded by 
adjoining heights from which the internal economy of the hamlet 
can be viewed, and a well-judged attack with firearms would render 
opposition useless. The houses are built in irregular lines facing 
inwards, with the eaves almost touching the ground; the front 
gable is from fifteen to forty feet in height, and the roof, which 1S 
made of grass and bamboos, slopes off in the rear to a height of 
from ten to fifteen feet. In width the houses vary from twenty to 
forty feet, and in length from about thirty to fifty or even sixty feet + 
in many villages each house is surrounded by a substantial stone 
wall. A dwelling-house generally consists of two apartments, In 
the front room the grain is usually stored away in huge bamboo 
baskets from five to ten feet in diameter; thick broad planks are 
placed around the room for sitting and sleeping upon. The second

THE RENGMA NAGAS. re

and they esteem the flesh of the elephant as a great delicacy. 
They secure their game by pitfall traps, covered over with 
branches and leaves of trees. ‘The bottom of the pit is filled with 
bamboo spikes, so that any animal falling into the pit is transfixed 
and killed. The only agricultural implements they possess are a 
heavy, long, square-headed déo or handbill, and a light hoe. The 
mode of cultivation followed by them is chiefly that known as jum, 
a system which necessitates fresh land being taken up every two or 
three years. But in the higher ranges, where the hills have a gentle 
slope, the sides are cut into terraces from the base to the summit, 
and the same land is continuously cultivated, the fields being irrigated 
by means of artificial channels, along which water is often conducted 
from long distances.

On the death of a person of consequence, no person leaves the 
village for three days, and the body iskept inthehouse. Atthe end 
of this period, a great feast of cows’ and pigs’ flesh and rice spirits 
is given to the whole community. The body is then conveyed to 
the burying-ground and interred, a stone tomb being built over the 
grave three or four feet high. Those present vociferously curse 
the evil spirit who has taken their comrade from them, and place 
on the grave all the articles of dress worn by the deceased, as well 
as his arms, drinking cup, and ornaments. A number of pigs’ and 
cows’ skulls are also stuck up on sticks at one end of the grave, 
in token of the hospitality of their departed friend:

Tus Rencmas, another cian of Ndgds, are a small, quiet, 
‘noffensive tribe, living in the hills of the same name between the 
Jamund and Kalidn{ rivers. At the present day they can with 
difficulty be distinguished from the Mikirs, in the vicinity of whom 
they live, and with whom many of them have intermarried. They 
are also now adopting the Mikir style of dress. Tradition states that 
they originally occupied the higher ranges east of the Dhaneswari,

but were forced to fly to their present homes in consequence of 
ks of other and more poweiful Naga

intestine feuds and the attac ef a 
tribes. ‘The Deputy-Commissioner thinks it probable that they are 
only a branch of the Angdimi Nagds. 4A council of village elders

decides all trival disputes, and imposes fines upon the offending party. 
Their villages are small, and with a few exceptions undefended, 
although from their being situated in the midst of heavy forest 
jungle and dense underwood without roads, they are most difficult 
of access. Besides rice, a considerable quantity of cotton 1s grown
